            Animation Viewing Guide
          Some of the geometric and algorithmic concepts in this thesis are illustrated with an-
          imated GIF files, so that the temporal behavior of algorithms and the evolution of
          geometric structures over time become easier to understand. Two ways of viewing these
          animations have been provided:
           Electronic (PDF file): by clicking on the Ŏ icon next to each reference, the
           animation’s web page opens in the browser and the animation plays automatically.
           Printed (paper version): by scanning the green QR code in the page margin with
           a smartphone camera, the same web page opens on the phone and the animation
           becomes viewable.
          To make this clear, the animations have been placed in exactly this form (QR code in
          the margin and clickable icon in the text), so that the reader can access the moving
          content directly in both the electronic and printed formats.
                                                        In the Name of God
                             Abstract
                       This thesis studies the kinetic covering problem in which n points, moving along known piecewise-
                       linear trajectories over a finite horizon [0,T], must be covered at every instant by variable-
                       radius disks centered at m fixed stations.   The objective is the cumulative covered area,
                       J =RT πPiri(t)2dt—theMinSumcriterion—indeliberatecontrasttothepeak-instantaneous-
                            0
                       area criterion of the antecedent literature. Within the primary literature inspected for this thesis,
                       the kinetic problem under this cumulative objective had not previously been analyzed; the claim
                       is bounded to the inspected corpus and is recorded with an explicit retraction clause. The analy-
                       sis is developed under the default model of time-dependent assignment with unrestricted radius
                       jumps; every decisive model decision is recorded as unresolved, and every claim is conditional
                       on that default. The main results are established with complete proofs: the pointwise optimum
                       is piecewise quadratic with finitely many switching instants, and its integral is attained by a
                       feasible schedule, so the optimum is a minimum rather than an infimum; the optima of the three
                       assignment–regularity regimes are partially ordered, and an instance-level analysis shows that
                       radius continuity costs attainment but not value; and exact optimization is NP-hard in every
                       regime. On this foundation, the thesis specifies a certified bounding scheme — closed-form inter-
                       val lower bounds valid in every single-coverage regime, integrated upper bounds, and a proved
                       global bracket over any refining partition — organized as an interval branch-and-bound over a
                       three-API oracle contract with status conditions that separate certified, numerically bounded,
                       heuristic, and sampled outputs, together with a product-form mixed-integer formulation proved
                       equivalent to the static subproblem. Convergence of the refinement loop is stated as a proof
                       sketch under idealized assumptions, and no polynomial-time claim is made. The evaluation pro-
                       tocol is drafted but not executed: the thesis contains no implementation and no experimental run,
                       and it makes no empirical claim. Exact analytic anchors on a running example — closed-form
                       optima, a hand-executed refinement trace, and the leaf-count estimate that fixed the registered
                       interval budget — are verified by direct computation and remain instance-specific. The thesis
                       closes with a dependency-ordered agenda for future work and an explicit evidence discipline that
                       keeps proved, sketched, instance-specific, and pending statements distinct.
